生産者物価も大幅上昇し、インフレへの警戒強まる

日本：7月以降の設備投資需要はAI関連産業を中心に旺盛
3
 7～9月期の機械受注は増勢回復へ。AI関連業種からの受注がけん引
― ①旺盛なAI需要のもと、関連業種では設備不足感が強まっていたこと、②輸出増が持続するもとで、好業績が期待され
ること、③中東情勢の不確実性が後退したことなどが背景
 先行き設備投資は緩やかな増勢を辿る見込み
― 半導体製造装置をはじめ、AI・データセンター関連品目を中心に受注残が増加しており、目先こうした傾向が続く見通し
― 積み上がった受注残を段階的に消化することで、設備投資が持続的に下支えされる公算が大きい

世界：AI関連投資の拡大が鉱物需要を押し上げる一方、鈍い資源開発投資の回復ペース
4
 AI関連需要の拡大が銅やレアアースの需給環境に影響
― データセンター建設や電力インフラ整備に伴い、銅やレアアースの需要が拡大。ガリウム等の戦略鉱物への需要も増加
 資源開発投資の回復が遅れるなか、中長期的な供給不足への懸念強まる
―IEAは、2035年に銅が供給量対比で約26％の供給不足に陥ると予測。レアアースは供給不足を回避する見込みながら、

採掘・精錬において中国が支配的地位を占めており、供給元の集中がリスク
IEA：銅、レアアースの需要見通し IEA：銅・レアアースの需給バランス予測（2035年）
（出所）IEA "Global Critical Minerals Outlook 2026"より、みずほ総合研究所調査部作成
AI関連投資の拡大を背景に、
銅やレアアースの需要が拡大
銅は供給が不足へ
レアアースは供給超過も供給元の集中がリスク
注目点①AI投資拡大と資源供給制約

世界：AI投資が金融市場を下支えする一方、持続性への懸念は残存
 IMFはAI投資や企業収益の拡大を背景に金融市場が底堅さを維持していると評価
― ただし、金融安定リスクは引き続き高いとの評価で、AI、国債市場、サイバーを主要なリスク要因として指摘
 AIについてはAI主導の市場調整リスクを警戒
―AI中核企業の収益拡大によって株価の割高感は緩和する一方、社債発行や循環的な資金調達が拡大。投資収益が期
待を下回った場合、金融市場への影響が増幅される可能性
5

世界：国債市場に残る脆弱性。インフレ・財政懸念による金利急騰リスクは増大
 主要国の長期金利は、期待短期金利とタームプレミアムの双方から上昇
―IMFは財政懸念などを背景にタームプレミアムが歴史的に高い領域にあると指摘
― 国債発行の増加や借換リスクの高まりに加え、価格感応度の高い投資家の存在がインフレ・財政ショックによる金利上
昇を増幅する可能性
― 国債市場は足元で秩序を維持するも、急激な金利上昇は資金調達市場の逼迫や銀行部門への波及を招くリスク
6

### Page 8

世界：ヘッジファンドのレバレッジ拡大が市場ショックを増幅、銀行部門への波及にも警戒
 高まるヘッジファンドの存在感と市場への影響力
― ヘッジファンドの総資産は2013年の約4兆ドルから2026年初には約13兆ドルに拡大。平時には市場の流動性や効率性
を支える一方、ストレス時にはレバレッジ解消や取引集中が価格変動を増幅させる懸念
 ヘッジファンドの損失がプライムブローカーを通じて銀行部門に波及するリスク
―IMFはヘッジファンドの運用成績悪化がプライムブローカーの収益にも悪影響を及ぼすと分析。銀行自身の健全性に加
え、ノンバンクとの相互連関を通じた金融システム全体への波及にも警鐘
7

世界：2026年の世界経済は底堅く推移も、経済成長の恩恵やインフレの負担は一部に偏在
 IMFは2026年の世界成長率を小幅に上方修正（3.0％→3.1％）
―AIブームを追い風とした半導体輸出の増加等を背景に、アジアを中心に上方修正
― 備蓄放出・代替調達等による政府・企業の対応が原油供給ショックによる下押しを緩和したことも影響
 一方、AI需要の取り込みや供給ショックへの耐性には国家間で格差
―IMFによれば、世界の経済成長の95％を占める国の数は54（4月）から32（10月）に減少したほか、今次改定では47％
の国が成長率を下方修正。AIブームの恩恵や供給ショックによる負担は一部の国・地域に偏在

（年／月）
日本：コメ価格下落で企業物価は鈍化。もっとも、先行きは原油高を背景に再加速へ
18
 9月の国内企業物価はコメ価格の下落を主因に、前年比＋6.8％（前月：同＋7.6％）と鈍化
― 新米価格が昨年を下回ったこと等を背景に、コメ価格が大幅に下落。国内企業物価を▲0.75%Pt押し下げ
― 一方、3月来の原油高を背景に化学・プラスチック製品は高止まり。また、AI需要等を背景に非鉄金属も高い伸びを維持
 7月以降の原油高再燃を背景に、先行きの国内企業物価は再加速する見込み
― 原油価格高止まりにタンカー運賃の急騰が重なり、先行きの輸入物価は原油関連を中心に加速へ
― 国内企業物価は石油・石炭製品やラグを伴いつつ化学・プラスチック製品などを中心に再加速する見込み
（注） コメは玄米及び精米。食品関連は、飲食料品及び農林水産物（除くコメ）

（出所） 日本銀行「企業物価指数」より、みずほ総合研究所調査部作成
国内企業物価 輸入物価（円建て）と国内企業物価
（注） 輸入物価は1カ月先行。原油輸入単価とドバイ原油（1カ月先行）を使用し、輸入物価（契
約通貨建て）の先行きを試算し、為替は1ドル157円程度の推移を想定。二重線部分は見
通し
（出所） 日本銀行「企業物価指数」、財務省「貿易統計」、IMF、LSEGより、みずほ総合研究所

### Page 1

Summary: Nanya Technology (Nanya) announced its 3Q26 results after market hours on 
12 October. Its revenue of TWD133.6m (+62% QoQ, +612% YoY) and operating profit of 
TWD106.9m (+76% QoQ, +95.5x YoY) were robust. While shipments decreased by a mid-
single-digit % QoQ, ASP increased by > 60% QoQ mainly due to renewed LTA contracts. 
While DDR4 accounted for >60% of revenue, management expects the favourable pricing 
environment to persist on robust demand and supply constraints. However, due to massive 
capex (c.USD16bn) on the build-out of a new 50k wpm capacity, management didn’t 
announce an enhanced shareholder return plan or a higher dividend payout.

Key highlights

• 3Q26 results review. Nanya's 3Q26 revenue of TWD133.6m (+62% QoQ, +612% YoY) 
and operating profit of TWD106.9m (+76% QoQ, +95.5x YoY) were slightly below our 
and recent market estimates due to higher employee compensation expenses. Its 
DRAM ASP increased by >60% QoQ in 3Q26 on LTA price adjustments, while 
shipments decreased by a mid-single-digit % QoQ. DDR5 accounted for >10% of 3Q26 
revenue, while DDR4 accounted for >60%. Management guided for a persistent market 
shortage and a continued price uptrend in 4Q26, while shipments are expected to 
remain flat until wafer output from the new fab comes online in 4Q27.

• Key takeaways from conference call. Management expects AI infrastructure to keep 
driving DRAM demand. While AI infrastructure and server applications currently account 
for >20% of revenue, it expects this exposure to grow to >30% through 2029-30. In 
particular, DDR4 demand now accounts for 12-14% of total DRAM market demand, 
maintaining the higher ASP trend on strong demand. With major DRAM players having 
almost completed their HBM supply negotiations for 2027, management believes it 
would be hard to increase supply given capacity allocation and longer lead times. Its 
LTA output portion was >20%, and management expects this to increase going forward. 
The company announced a new fab construction plan for 30k wpm by the end of 2028 
and up to 50k wpm by 2030 to respond to LTA demand from AI infrastructure. Despite 
the solid 3Q26 earnings, there was no announcement of an increased dividend payout, 
due to the capex burden (c.USD16bn for 50k wpm). It did not guide for any share 
buyback.

Recommendation 
We have a Buy (1) call on Nanya with a 12M TP of TWD700, based on a 7.4x PER on the 
2026-27E EPS. While the robust 3Q26 earnings led by the significant ASP hike was 
positive, we view the delay in announcing a shareholder return plan as disappointing. Key 
risk: weaker-than-expected commodity DRAM demand due to delays in data centre build-
outs.

Country

Shipments -2% vs. MSe;
Lowering 4Qe by 3%
Morgan Stanley Taiwan Limited+
Howard Kao

Greater China Technology Hardware
Asia Pacific
Industry View In-Line
Overall September ODM NB shipments came in 2% below MSe: Quarter-end pull-
ins drove notebook shipments up 32% m/m, although volumes were 2% below MSe,
with weakness mainly coming from Wistron and Compal, and to a lesser extent,
Pegatron, reflecting slower NB shipments from Dell, as Dell accounted for ~60% of

Compal's NB shipment, and ~55% of Wistron's in CY25, on our estimates. Thus total
3Q NB builds came in at 25.2M units (-17% q/q, -26% y/y). This sequential decline
is sub-seasonal vs. the 15Y average seasonality of +5% q/q in 3Q.
Pull-forward demand appears to have come to an end, channel partners and
distributors have stocked up on inventory, and OEMs are slowing their sales into the
channel. We believe this is why our conversations with the ODMs suggest a slightly

weaker 4Q volume outlook than previously expected, despite favorable mix shift.
Thus, we lower our 4Q NB build estimate by 3% to 23.1M (-8% q/q, -29% y/y).
This sequential decline is sub-seasonal (despite an already lowered base in 3Q) vs.
the 15Y average seasonality of flat q/q in 4Q. We forecast October NB builds at
6.8M (-30% m/m, -33% y/y). Our revised forecasts imply CY26 builds of ~107.8M
units (-16% y/y) for the top five Taiwan NB ODMs.

Our top five ODMs' CY26 NB build estimate is ~107.8M units (-16% y/y).

STOCK RATINGS
Morgan Stanley uses a relative rating system using terms such as Overweight, Equal-weight, Not-Rated or Underweight (see definitions below). Morgan Stanley does not assign ratings of Buy,
Hold or Sell to the stocks we cover. Overweight, Equal-weight, Not-Rated and Underweight are not the equivalent of buy, hold and sell.  Investors should carefully read the definitions of all
ratings used in Morgan Stanley Research. In addition, since Morgan Stanley Research contains more complete information concerning the analyst's views, investors should carefully read Morgan

Stanley Research, in its entirety, and not infer the contents from the rating alone.  In any case, ratings (or research) should not be used or relied upon as investment advice.  An investor's decision
to buy or sell a stock should depend on individual circumstances (such as the investor's existing holdings) and other considerations.

Analyst Stock Ratings
Overweight (O). The stock's total return is expected to exceed the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next
12-18 months.
Equal-weight (E). The stock's total return is expected to be in line with the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over

the next 12-18 months.
Not-Rated (NR). Currently the analyst does not have adequate conviction about the stock's total return relative to the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage
universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next 12-18 months.
Underweight (U). The stock's total return is expected to be below the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next

12-18 months.
Unless otherwise specified, the time frame for price targets included in Morgan Stanley Research is 12 to 18 months.
Analyst Industry Views
Attractive (A): The analyst expects the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months to be attractive vs. the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated
below.
In-Line (I): The analyst expects the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months to be in line with the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated below.

Cautious (C): The analyst views the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months with caution vs. the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated below.
Benchmarks for each region are as follows: North America - S&P 500; Latin America - relevant MSCI country index or MSCI Latin America Index; Europe - MSCI Europe; Japan - TOPIX; Asia -
relevant MSCI country index or MSCI sub-regional index or MSCI AC Asia Pacific ex Japan Index.

Gogolook三大事業體
資料來源：Gogolook、群益投顧彙整
⚫ Gogolook 是全球領先的信任科技 (TrustTech) 公司，運用自有防詐資料庫與 AI 技
術，提供消費者防詐、企業端防詐與金融科技服務。Gogolook 擁有全球最齊全的
通訊與數位防詐數據資料庫，涵蓋電話號碼、網址、和虛擬貨幣錢包地址等。

1,800萬，主要服務市場

### Page 7

7
whoscall海外市場持續擴張
⚫ whoscall海外市場擴張成效卓越，海外營收占比於2025年已突破63%。泰國為
Gogolook最大的海外市場，其用戶規模、營收貢獻已超越台灣，但仍具備很大的
成長空間；公司於曼谷擁有龐大用戶基礎，目前正進軍泰國二、三線城市。菲律
賓與馬來西亞等地已具備百萬等級的活躍用戶，後續成長可期。
⚫ Gogolook於09/2026已正式和泰國政府退休基金(Government Pension Fund，GPF)簽
署MOU，並與國家跨行交易管理與交換公司(National ITMX)及泰國存款保障機構
(Deposit Protection Agency，DPA)合作，將whoscall防詐服務導入GPF體系，涵蓋
退休人員及現職公務員(超過120萬名會員)。此合作有助於同步推廣泰國當地B2C、
B2B業務。
⚫ 為了有效控制獲客成本，Gogolook在海外減少依賴成效較低的數位廣告，轉而與
當地政府、銀行及電信商合作。此外，考量到東南亞信用卡普及率較低，

Gogolook在泰國率先推出的「電信帳單扣款」訂閱模式大獲成功，目前此模式也
與台灣遠傳電信合作，藉此攻佔中高年齡層客群。

Gogolook收購ScamAdviser
⚫ 企業端防詐方面，由於各國法規有加重企業防詐責任的趨勢，Gogolook將此業務
視為長期成長動能潛力來源。Gogolook於2024年收購具備超過6,000萬筆網域資料
庫的ScamAdviser後，成功拓展歐美市場。儘管2025年因主動調整部分低毛利率業
務，導致ScamAdviser營收下滑，但其實質獲利反而增加。
資料來源：Gogolook、群益投顧彙整
⚫ ScamAdviser成立於荷蘭阿姆斯
特丹，為全球防詐聯盟GASA
基石成員。ScamAdviser以AI演
算法與資料庫為基礎，開發自
有的網站風險評級技術，與
Gogolook的企業防詐解決方案
與電話號碼資料庫整合，為全
球最完整的通訊與數位防詐數
據資料庫。

### Page 11

11
Gogolook企業端防詐服務
⚫ 企業端服務主要分為「資料庫授權」與「商譽保護」兩大主軸：(1) 防詐情資方案，
為一種資料庫授權模式，將whoscall的「電話/簡訊」資料庫以及ScamAdviser的
「網域」資料庫，透過API、SDK方式授權給企業。(2) 商譽保護服務，企業的外
部防詐監控中心，協助企業或政府主動在網路上巡邏，偵測並下架冒用其品牌的
假投資廣告、假粉專或釣魚網站。
資料來源：Gogolook、群益投顧彙整

### Page 12

12
Agent looker
⚫ 因應Agentic AI時代來臨，Gogolook於09/2026推出新產品Agent looker，工作原理
是在AI agent實際造訪或讀取網頁前，即時進行風險檢測並攔截惡意請求，可有效
避免AI agent被詐騙的風險。
⚫ 核心防護機制與功能：(1)毫秒級即時掃描，當AI Agent讀取外部網頁或執行URL
請求時，Agent Looker會在毫秒內對網頁封包與內容進行深度檢查，攔截惡意腳本、
釣魚網站以及針對 AI 模型的提示詞注入(Prompt Injection)攻擊。(2)Gogolook威脅
情資資料庫驅動。系統底層串接 Gogolook 累積多年的全球防詐騙、釣魚網站與風
險網域黑名單。基於海量真實風險樣本的數據庫，使其能快速且精準地辨識異常
行為。(3)活動稽核日誌。提供視覺化的管理儀表板，完整記錄AI Agent的所有外
部網路互動軌跡，包含造訪過的網址、讀取的網頁片段及最終的安全判定結果，
確保AI運作透明且可追溯。
⚫ Agent looker目前處於封閉測試(Private Beta)階段。

資料來源：Gogolook、群益投顧彙整

### Page 13

13
金融科技-袋鼠金融
⚫ 金融科技為Gogolook於疫情期間因應線上詐騙激增而開創的新業務，包含全台灣
流量最大的金融媒合平台「袋鼠金融Roo.Cash」，以及專攻小額借款的「JUJI招財
麻吉」。袋鼠金融 Roo.Cash與國內金融機構合作，提供200件以上的金融商品方案
智慧比較與媒合。智能問答服務「Roo AI」，可協助用戶在信用卡、貸款、保險、
投資理財與民生優惠資訊中，快速找到最適合的選項。若順利成交，Gogolook即
可藉此獲得媒合收入。
資料來源：Gogolook、群益投顧彙整

金融科技-JUJI招財麻吉

AI風控模型，不僅可嚴格控制核貸率，更將90天延滯率壓在極低水準。目前JUJI
招財麻吉應收分期款餘額持續增加，公司同時也把純風控模型顧問服務輸出至海

Source PDF: all report pdf/9月ＦＯＭＣ議事録：タカ派姿勢に滲む「データ依存」の慎重さ ～FRBは年内追加利上げを視野に入れるも、決定は今後の経済指標次第～.pdf
Report source: DLRI
Date: 2026-10-08

### Page 1

FED  Watching

2026 年 10 月 8 日

1/5 
9 月ＦＯＭＣ議事録：タカ派姿勢に滲む「データ依存」の慎重さ 
～FRB は年内追加利上げを視野に入れるも、決定は今後の経済指標次第～ 
 経済調査部 主任エコノミスト  桂畑 誠治  
（℡：050-5474-7493）

要旨  
・9 月FOMCでは全会一致（12 対 0）で 0.25%の利上げを決定。非投票メンバーを含む参加者 
全 19 人が利上げに賛成した。 
・経済・金利見通し（SEP）提出者の大半が年内（10 月または 12 月）の更なる利上げを見込 
んでおり、全体としてタカ派的トーンが際立つ。 
・インフレリスクは上振れ傾向にあるものの、中長期のインフレ予想は 2%目標水準に安定。 
FRBは毎会合のデータ次第で柔軟に判断する「オープンな姿勢」を維持している。 
・今後の見通しとしては、景気や労働市場が安定する中でコアCPIやコアPCEの鈍化が確認され 
れば、予防的な追加利上げが見送られる可能性がある（最終決定は 12 月FOMCまでのデータ次
第） 。

１． 9 月FOMC ：参加者 19 人全員 が利上げ に賛成  
ケビン・ウォーシュFRB議長の新体制下で初めての利上げが決定された 9 月FOMCの議事録が公表 
された。新体制では会合直後の声明文のテキスト量が大幅に削減されたほか、記者会見時間も短
縮され、直接的な情報発信量が絞り込まれている。そうした中で、後日公表される「議事録」の
情報量は引き続き高水準に維持されており、FRBの本音を推し量る最重要ツールとしての重みが増
している。ただし、公表がFOMC終了から 3 週間後となるため、情報の遅効性には注意が必要であ 
る。 
9 月の利上げ決定については、投票権のないメンバーも含めた参加者全員（19 人）が利上げに賛
成した。さらに、経済・金利予測（SEP：議長自身は今回も SEP を提出していない）で示されていた 
通り、SEP を提出した 18 人の参加者のうち大半が「年内（10 月 27-28 日または 12 月 15-16 日）に
更なる利上げが適切となる可能性が高い」と見解を示しており、インフレへの強い警戒感を示すタ 
カ派（金融引き締め寄り）なトーンが際立つものとなった。ただし、委員会として今後の政策判断 
は新たに入手されるデータに依存（Data-dependent）するスタンスを明確に維持している。

２．労働市場は完全雇用水準で安定、インフレには上振れリスク 
委員会は全会一致（12 対 0）で、FF金利誘導目標レンジを 0.25％ポイント引き上げ、3.75％〜

### Page 2

2/5 
4.00％とすることを決定した。労働市場が完全雇用に近い水準で安定している一方で、インフレ 
率が高止まりしていることが背景にある。 
インフレの判断では、参加者は共通して、 「インフレ率は高止まりしており、ここ数ヶ月でイン 
フレ抑制に向けた十分な進展は見られていない」と指摘した。 「原油や精製燃料製品の価格を押し 
上げた継続的な地政学的動向や、急増するAI関連投資がインフレ圧力に寄与している」との認識 
が示された。 
複数の参加者がコア財・コアサービス価格の上振れや、関税の影響に言及したほか、数名の参 
加者がPCE指数の算出手法改定による影響や、3 ヶ月年率換算のインフレ率が年後半にインフレを 
過小評価しやすい傾向に留意すべきだと警鐘を鳴らした。一方で参加者全員が、 「中長期のインフ 
レ予想に関する市場ベースおよび調査ベースの指標が、委員会の 2％目標と一致する水準を維持し 
ている」と判断しており、追加利上げを過度に急ぐ必要性は示されなかった。 
労働市場に関しては、参加者は概ね「労働市場の状況が安定している」と判断し、 「完全雇用に 
近い状態にある」と評価した。 「失業率が現在の水準付近にとどまり、労働市場の状況は引き続き 
安定する」と予想した。一方で、数名の参加者が低水準の採用・解雇率など流動性の低下を指摘 
し、一部の参加者がAI専門職における局所的な賃金上昇圧力に触れた。そのうえで、参加者は概
ね「労働市場に対する上振れリスクと下振れリスクはほぼ均衡している」と評価した。 
経済活動に関しても、参加者は一般に「堅調なペースで拡大している」と評価。 「地政学的動向 
による供給面での悪影響が強まったものの、旺盛な設備投資と底堅い個人消費が経済活動を支え 
ている」との判断が示された。なお、一部の参加者からは、AI関連投資が設備投資と潜在生産性 
を押し上げる一方で中期的に総需要が総供給を上回るリスクや、株高などを背景とする金融環境 
の緩和的状況、高所得層と低・中所得層における消費の二極化構造への懸念が指摘された。 
以上のように、参加者は共通して「労働市場が完全雇用に近く力強さの兆候も見られる一方で 
インフレ率は高止まりしており、経済活動が堅調なペースで拡大していること」を強調した。さ
らに、ほぼすべての参加者が、 「インフレリスクは上振れに傾いているものの、労働市場に対する 
リスクは減少し、現在はほぼ均衡している」と評価した。こうした経済見通しと変化するリスク 
バランスに基づき、全参加者（19 人）がFF金利誘導目標レンジの引き上げが適切であると判断し
た。

3．政策金利の水準感とリスク管理の姿勢 
今回の議論において注目されるのは、利上げの根拠と政策金利水準に対する見解の多様さであ 
る。多くの参加者は、予想以上の需要や供給面での悪影響によりインフレ率が持続的に目標を上 
回り続けることに対する保険（Insurance）を提供するため、誘導目標レンジを高めの軌道とする 
ことがリスク管理上の観点から賢明であると強調した。 
一方で、相当数の参加者はリスク管理上の観点からではなく、自身の標準的な見通し（Modal 
outlooks）に基づいて高めの目標軌道が必要であると見なした。さらに、数名の参加者は中立金 
利の推計値を引き上げたことに触れ、現在の政策金利は「緊縮的ではない、あるいは極めて緩や

当化する材料となる。 
また、バランスシート政策に関しては、数名の参加者が国債市場の円滑な機能を認めつつも、 
将来の市場不全に備えたツールやコミュニケーションの強化を提案したことが確認された。

４．先行きに対する見通し 
今回の会合以降の金融政策の見通しに関して、大半の参加者（SEP提出者 18 人のうち）が年末 
までにFF金利誘導目標レンジを再度引き上げることが適切となる可能性が高いとの見方を示し 
た。ただし、全員が共通のスタンスとしてオープンな姿勢で各会合に臨み、今後の決定は新たに 
入手する情報と、それが経済見通しおよびリスクバランスに与える含意に依存することを強調、
毎会合ごとに判断されることを示し、政策の自由度を維持した。 
今回の議事録は全体として「年内追加利上げ」に前向きなタカ派的トーンが目立ったものの、 
インフレ予想の安定や「毎会合データ次第」というスタンスから、FRBが無理な連続利上げを焦っ 
ているわけではないことが示された。今後の金融政策の焦点を左右するのは、10 月・12 月FOMCま
でに発表される景気、労働市場や、コアCPI、コアPCEの動向である。景気や労働市場の安定が続 
くと予想されるもとで、インフレの鈍化傾向が確認される場合、予防的な追加利上げは見送られ 
る可能性がある（最終決定は今後のデータ次第） 。

### Page 1

현대차 (005380)

EARNINGS PREVIEW Company Analysis | 자동차/로봇 | 2026.10.16  
4분기 반등 기대

3Q26 Preview 
현대차는 3Q26E 매출액 42.4조원(-9.4% YoY, -13.9% QoQ), 영업이익 2.5조원(-
2.8% YoY, -13.5% QoQ, OPM 5.8%)을 기록, 컨센서스를 하회할 전망이다. 각 부문
별 영업이익은 자동차 1.7조원(-2.0% YoY, -14.9% QoQ, OPM 4.8%), 금융 5,501억
원(-4.5% YoY, -27.5% QoQ, OPM 6.9%)으로 예상한다. 
생산 정상화 및 신차 확대를 통한 수익성 개선 기대 
현대차는 글로벌 도매 판매 91.8만대(-11.5% 이하 YoY), 중국 제외 판매량은 90.9
만대(-9.6%)를 기록했다. 7~8월에 발생한 파업 영향으로 약 5.5만대 규모의 생산 차
질이 발생했다. 파업 영향으로 HEV 판매 증가율이 1%에 그치면서, HEV 판매 확대에 
따른 믹스 개선 효과도 시장 기대에 미치지 못했다.  
4분기부터는 3분기 파업 영향으로 지연된 생산량 회복 및 신차 확대가 본격화되면서, 
전분기 대비 매출 및 수익성 개선이 기대된다. 동사는 보급형 전기차 아이오닉3의 튀
르키예 생산을 9월부터 시작했으며, 이를 통해 유럽 시장 판매량은 점차 회복될 전망
이다. 또한, 1) HMGMA를 통한 HEV 미국 현지 생산 확대, 2) 제네시스 HEV 모델 순
차적 출시를 통해 내년부터 북미를 중심으로 믹스 개선이 본격화될 전망이다. 
투자의견은 Buy를 유지하고 목표주가는 520,000원으로 하향 
2027년 영업이익은 11.2조원(+10.2%)를 기록할 것으로 예상된다. 원화 약세 지속을 
반영해 이익 추정치를 하향하며, 이에 따라 목표주가를 하향한다. 다만, 현재 주가는 
12M Fwd P/E 기준 6.1배로 Legacy OEM 평균(7.7배)보다 낮은 수준이다. 1) RMAC 
성과 구체화, 2) RA(로봇 생산법인) 착공 등 Physical AI 사업이 구체화될 경우 밸류에
이션 할증이 가능할 전망이다. 
 Analyst 이병근 
bglee@ls-sec.co.kr 
Buy (유지)

 절대 (1.8) (21.8) 231.1   
 상대 (3.2) 1.6  63.7   
 절대 (달러환산) 0.3  (11.5) 239.5

3Q26 전사 예상 영업이익 100조원 
DS부문 예상 영업이익은 100.0조원(OPM 67.4%, YoY +1538.5%, QoQ +14.5%) 으로, 
달러환율 1,380원기준, 메모리와 비메모리 사업부문 예상 영업손익은 각각 102.0조원,    
-2.0조원으로 추정한다. 1) 메모리반도체 가격 상승세가 이어지는 가운데, HBM4 판매 
본격화에 따른 영향을 고려하면 DRAM/NAND 분기 평균 가격 상승폭은 각각 +18.0%, 
+16.0%으로 추정하고, 2)비메모리반도체 사업 적자는 지속될 전망이기 때문이다. 다만 
특별 성과급 지급에 따른 충당금 영향을 제외하고 선단공정 중심의 파운드리 가동률을 
고려하면 적자폭은 유의미하게 감소한 것으로 파악된다는 점이 긍정적이다.   
SDC부문 예상 영업이익은 1.1조원(OPM 13.0%, YoY -9.6%, QoQ +54.9%)으로, 1)국내 
주력 고객사향 폴더블 스마트폰 판매 호조가 사업부의 외형 성장에 긍정적으로 작용했을 
것으로 추정됨에도 불구하고, 2) 8.6세대 IT OLED 패널 양산 개시에 따른 감가상각비(약 
0.3조원 수준)와 북미 고객 중심의 판가 인하 압박 영향으로 전년동기대비 수익성은 약 
2%pt 훼손이 불가피할 전망이다. DX부문 예상 영업손익은 -1.1조원(YoY 적자전환, QoQ 
적자지속)으로, 메모리반도체를 포함한 IT 부품 가격 부담이 전분기대비 확대되고 있기 
때문이다. MX와 VD/DA사업부 예상 손익은 각각 -0.9조원, -0.2조원으로 추정한다.

투자의견 BUY, 목표주가 63만원으로 상향 조정  
메모리반도체의 가격 상승폭이 다소 둔화되겠지만, 2027~2028년에도 타이트한 수급 
환경이 이어질 전망이라는 점을 주목한다. 1)시장은 신규 증설과 HBM 관련 노이즈로 인한 
2028년 피크아웃 가능성을 우려하고 있으나, HBM 의 범용 DRAM 생산능력 잠식과 AI 
추론 확산에 따른 고용량 메모리 수요를 감안하면 공급 제약의 해소 시점은 예상보다 
늦어질 가능성이 높기 때문이다. 뿐만 아니라, 2)메모리 업사이클이 장기화될 경우 동사 
FCF는 과거대비 구조적으로 확대되어 보다 친화적인 주주환원 정책(2027~2029년, 
2027년 1월 예정)을 제시할 가능성도 배제할 수 없기 때문이다. 동사 2027년 연간 실적 
추정치 상향 조정에 따라 목표주가를 63만원(2027년 BPS * Target PBR 3.5배 적용)으로 
상향 조정한다. 피크아웃 우려에 따른 주가 조정은 중장기 관점에서 매수 기회다.

 상대 (14.8) 6.0  (53.4)  
 절대 (달러환산) (7.9) 11.8  (4.8)

3Q26Pre: 환율 하락에도 성장 흐름 유지 
매출액 1조 3,477억원(+7.2%YoY, 이하 YoY 생략), 영업이익 5,957억원(-6.0%, OPM 
44.2%)로 컨센서스에 부합할 것으로 전망. 3분기 원달러 평균 환율은 1,423원으로 전분기 
평균 환율 대비 약 5.2% 하락에도 불구하고 성장 흐름 지속. 
2분기 노조 파업으로 인한 영향은 3분기 생산을 진행했으며 4분기 매출에 반영될 예정으로 
연간 실적에 미치는 영향은 제한적일 것으로 예상. GSK로부터 인수한 록빌 공장도 안정적
으로 생산이 이루어지고 있으며 CAPA 여유분은 추가 수주를 진행 중으로 28~29년 이후 
완전 가동 전망. 
4Q, 6공장 건설과 폴리펩타이드 인수 
유상 증자 관련 투자 설명서 상 투자 계획에 따르면 6공장 투자는 26년부터 29년까지 진행 
될 예정으로 29년 가동 목표. 6공장은 5공장과 동일한 18만 리터 규모로 건설되며, 연내 투
자 집행을 시작함에 따라 건설 발표도 임박한 것으로 추정. 동사는 일정 수준 이상 기존 공
장 수주를 확보한 후 후속 공장 건설을 진행해오고 있어 non-binding 수주 계약의 본계약 
전환도 순차적으로 이루어질 것으로 전망. 
폴리펩타이드 인수도 현재 공개 매수를 진행 중에 있으며 연내 마무리될 것으로 예상. 
1H26 폴리펩타이드 매출은 2.37억 유로(+41.6%)로 GLP-1 agonist 등 펩타이드 의약품 
수요 증가에 따라 높은 성장 지속. 
투자 의견 Buy, 목표 주가 200만원으로 하향 
매수 의견을 유지하며 목표 주가는 기존 210만원에서 200만원으로 하향. 목표 주가 하향은 
유상 증자에 따른 EPS 희석에 기인. 글로벌 항체 CDMO 수요가 여전히 견조하며, 폴리펩
타이드 인수로 펩타이드로의 확장에 따른 추가적인 성장 기대. 주주 대상 유상 증자 영향과 
파업 우려 등이 있으나 노사 합의 시 주가 반등 전망.

Appendix

Document 261: Anthropic推出全天候AI智能体Claude Duet，Opus 5.5 驱动

Source PDF: all report pdf/Anthropic推出全天候AI智能体Claude Duet，Opus 5.5 驱动.pdf
Report source: WallstreetCN (Global)
Date: 2026-10-21
Pages: 3
Status: ok

### Page 1

Anthropic 表⽰， Claude Duet ⾯向⼯作任务繁重的专业⼈⼠和⼩企业主设计，定位为能够
在后台持续运⾏、主动识别⽤⼾需求并执⾏⼯作任务的 AI 智能体。⽬前产品仍处于研究预
览阶段，仅通过候补名单向美国地区的 Claude Pro 和 Max 订阅⽤⼾开放。
摘要：
Anthropic 正将 AI 竞争从模型能⼒延伸⾄任务执⾏。
当地时间周三，公司推出新应⽤ Claude Duet ，将其定位为能够在后台持续运⾏、主动识别⽤⼾
需求并执⾏⼯作任务的 AI 智能体，进⼀步拓展 Claude 的产品形态。
与等待⽤⼾发出指令的传统对话式助⼿不同， Claude Duet 能够汇总关联应⽤中的重要信息，在
需要⽤⼾关注时主动发送通知，并处理从会议安排到财务预测、⽹⻚应⽤开发等多种任务。
不过，这款产品⽬前仍处于研究预览阶段，仅通过候补名单向美国地区的 Claude Pro 和 Max 订
阅⽤⼾开放。有限的开放范围意味着， Claude Duet 尚未进⼊⾯向更⼴泛⽤⼾群体的全⾯竞争阶

段。
Anthropic 表⽰， Claude Duet 基于其旗舰模型 Claude Opus 5.5 开发，可在⽤⼾离开设备后继续
执⾏任务，但在判断信息紧急程度和处理部分个⼈事务⽅⾯仍存在局限。随着 Meta 和 OpenAI 也
在推进主动式 AI 智能体， Anthropic 正尝试将模型能⼒转化为⽤⼾服务。
从响应指令转向主动执⾏
根据 Anthropic 发布的官⽅公告， Claude Duet ⾯向⼯作任务繁重的专业⼈⼠和⼩企业主设计。
应⽤每天早晨会汇总关联应⽤中的重要事项，并在⼀天中持续检查相关信息，在发现需要⽤⼾
处理的事项时主动发出提醒。
⽤⼾也可以直接交办任务。除安排会议等⽇常⾏政⼯作外， Claude Duet 还能够借助多个⼯具构
建仪表盘、制作财务或销售预测、开发原型及⽹⻚应⽤。
其核⼼变化在于，⽤⼾不必始终停留在对话界⾯中逐步下达指令，智能体可以在后台推进任
务，并在完成或需要审核时通知⽤⼾。
Anthropic 称， Claude Duet 继承⽤⼾ Claude 账⼾中的记忆、技能和已连接应⽤，减少重复配置

的需要。产品提供独⽴的 iOS 和 Android 应⽤，也可通过⽹⻚端使⽤。
依托 Opus 5.5 ，连接逾 6000 种⼯具
Claude Duet 以 Claude Opus 5.5 为基础，并配备独⽴虚拟机和浏览器环境，使其能够在⽤⼾的
电脑或⼿机关闭后继续⼯作。
根据 Anthropic 的介绍，产品可以调⽤ Claude Marketplace 中超过 6000 种连接器和插件，覆盖
Gmail 、 Slack 等⼯作⼯具，以及 QuickBooks 、 Stripe 等业务服务。
这些连接器使智能体能够将信息检索与后续操作结合起来。例如，它可以检查未处理的邮件和
未付款发票、起草提醒信息、准备薪资处理内容，也可以协助制作营销材料、管理⽹店或准备
⾸⻚ 资讯 快讯 ⾏情 ⽇历 APP VIP 会员 ⼤师课 ⻅闻历 搜索

中的任何意⻅、观点或结论是否符合其特定状况。据此投资，责任⾃负。

⽀付和信息发送等关键步骤仍受到审批机制约束。 Anthropic 表⽰， Claude Duet 在发送消息、付
款、签署⽂件或删除信息前，会向⽤⼾展⽰拟执⾏的操作并等待批准。
产品也设置了专⻔的安全机制，以防⽌敏感信息被不当披露；部分⾼⻛险操作，即使获得⽤⼾
批准也⽆法执⾏。
研究预览阶段，能⼒与使⽤范围仍有限
Claude Duet ⽬前并⾮全⾯开放的商业产品。 Anthropic 此次⾸先向美国地区的 Pro 和 Max 订阅⽤
⼾开放候补申请，研究预览期间，智能体与 Claude 其他功能共享订阅使⽤额度，后台检查和复
杂任务同样会消耗额度。
Anthropic 也承认，现阶段产品仍可能误读对话内容或错误判断事项的紧急程度。由于产品主要
围绕⼯作场景开发，其处理订票等个⼈事务的能⼒相对较弱，⽤⼾⽬前也⽆法直接查看其浏览

器操作过程。
因此， Claude Duet 的实际价值仍取决于它能否在持续获取信息、主动判断任务优先级和执⾏操
作之间形成稳定的⼯作流程。对于企业和专业⽤⼾⽽⾔，后台⾃动化有望减少信息筛选和重复
操作，但判断失误与敏感信息处理仍是使⽤过程中需要关注的问题。
AI 智能体竞争进⼀步延伸
Anthropic 推出 Claude Duet 之际， OpenAI 和 Meta 也在推进能够代表⽤⼾执⾏任务的 AI 智能体。
OpenAI 此前推出了 Dots ， Meta 则推出了 Muse ，相关产品均试图让 AI 从回答问题进⼀步⾛向跨
应⽤完成任务。
The Verge 将 Claude Duet 视为 Meta Muse 和 OpenAI Dots 的竞争对⼿，并指出， Duet 整合了
Claude ⽣态中的记忆、技能与应⽤连接能⼒，通过虚拟机和浏览器执⾏⼯作任务。
Business Insider 则指出， Claude Duet ⽬前仍处于候补名单开放阶段，尚不能⽴即⾯向更⼴泛
的市场与其他智能体产品展开直接竞争。

对 Anthropic ⽽⾔， Claude Duet 不仅是 Claude 应⽤形态的扩展，也意味着公司正在探索如何让
模型持续参与⽤⼾的⽇常⼯作，⽽不只是提供⼀次性的问答和内容⽣成服务。其后续发展将取
决于任务执⾏的可靠性、⽤⼾对主动式服务的接受程度，以及产品能否从有限的研究预览逐步
扩⼤使⽤范围。

Document 314: Europe is de-risking without deglobalising

Source PDF: all report pdf/Europe is de-risking without deglobalising.pdf
Report source: ING Think
Date: 2026-10-21
Pages: 10
Status: ok

### Page 1

European ﬁrms have not reshored production as expected.
Instead, M&A deals point to greater control over supply chains,
especially in strategic industries. Cross-border links remain, but
sustaining them requires more investment and could make
resilience costlier
Pablo Muylle
European ﬁrms are strengthening control over cross-border supply chains
rather than reshoring
Supply chain risk has moved up the policy
agenda
The pressures on European supply chains have changed since the

Covid pandemic.
Logistics disruptions and input shortages have become less
prominent, while tariﬀs, regulatory requirements and geopolitical
uncertainty increasingly shape companies’ supply-chain decisions.
Recent evidence from the European Investment Bank reinforces this
shift: reported obstacles related to raw materials, semiconductors

### Page 2

and logistics declined between 2023 and 2025, while regulatory
compliance and customs and tariﬀ changes emerged as prominent
concerns.
Tariffs and regulation have become
prominent supply chain issues
Share of EU ﬁrms reporting selected obstacles; 2023 and 2025 where
available.
Source: EIB Supply Chain Survey, ING Economic Research
Reducing strategic dependencies has also become an explicit part
of the EU’s competitiveness agenda. In its 16 October conclusions,

the European Council called for renewed eﬀorts to reduce
dependencies alongside measures to promote industrial renewal,
innovation and investment.
This reﬂects a broader shift in European economic policy. Supply
chain resilience is increasingly being considered alongside
industrial renewal, innovation and competitiveness. The challenge
is to reduce critical dependencies without triggering a broader
retreat from international trade.
Despite this growing policy focus, the corporate response has not

centred on bringing production home. Previous ING research found
that reshoring accounted for less than 1% of recorded euro-area
restructuring events since 2019, compared with around 4% for
oﬀshoring.

### Page 3

But relocation is not the only way to reorganise a supply chain.
Companies can also seek greater control by acquiring suppliers or
ﬁrms operating at another stage of production. Production may
remain in the same place, and goods may continue to cross the
same borders, but the relationship changes from an arm’s-length
transaction to one in which the buyer has greater inﬂuence.
This distinction matters for the macroeconomic consequences of
de-risking. Reshoring changes the geography of production,

employment and trade. Vertical integration can leave these
patterns largely intact, while requiring ﬁrms to commit more capital
and giving them greater inﬂuence over investment, capacity and
access to inputs.
De-risking through ownership may therefore increase corporate
ﬁnancing needs without producing a comparable rise in domestic
output or employment. Its macroeconomic footprint could increase
less through the relocation of production and more through capital

allocation, ﬁnancing requirements and potentially higher
production costs. Supply chains may become more controlled and
more regional without European economies necessarily becoming
more self-suﬃcient.
To see whether ﬁrms are responding in this way, we have analysed
M&A transactions by non-ﬁnancial EU companies.
Vertical acquisitions have become more
common
Our results show a clear increase in vertically related acquisitions
since the middle of the last decade. A deal is considered vertically

related when the acquirer and target operate at diﬀerent stages of
the same supply chain.
On a 12-month moving-average basis, the share of such deals fell
to around 12% in 2015 before rising steadily. It reached almost
18% in 2023 and, after subsequently easing, stands at roughly
16% to 17% in the latest observations.

### Page 4

The vertical share of European acquisitions
has risen since 2015
Vertically related deals as a share of acquisitions by EU non-ﬁnancial
ﬁrms, 12-month average. A deal is considered vertically related when the
acquirer and target operate at diﬀerent stages of the same supply chain,
deﬁned as an input-output link of at least 5% between their industries.
Source: LSEG Workspace, Eurostat FIGARO input-output tables, ING
Economic Research
The upward evolution predates the pandemic, suggesting that the

shift towards greater control began before supply-chain resilience
became a prominent boardroom concern. Nevertheless, the
increase accelerated during a period in which companies faced
repeated disruptions and governments attached greater strategic
importance to supply chains.
The result should still be interpreted carefully. A vertical acquisition
does not always mean that a company is securing a critical
supplier. Some transactions, for example, reﬂect ordinary

consolidation, technological change or the pursuit of commercial
synergies. The aggregate trend, however, suggests that deals
connecting diﬀerent stages of production have become a more
important part of European M&A activity.
Deal counts provide the clearest signal. Deal values show a similar
pattern but are more volatile because many values are undisclosed,
and a few large transactions can dominate the results. We therefore
use values only as supporting evidence.

### Page 5

Strategic industries have a persistently
higher vertical deal share
The rise in vertical integration is not conﬁned to strategic sectors.
For this analysis, we deﬁne strategic industries as critical materials
and metals, energy, chemicals and pharmaceuticals, electronics
and electrical equipment, and machinery.
Other industries also recorded a meaningful increase, pointing to a
broader change in how European companies organise production.
Yet vertically related acquisitions remain consistently more

common in strategic industries. Their share rose from 16.6% in
2010-14 to 21.6% in 2023-25, compared with an increase from
12.0% to 15.6% in other industries.
Vertically related acquisitions are
consistently more common in strategic
industries
Vertically related deals as a share of acquisitions, pooled period averages.
Strategic industries comprise critical materials and metals, energy,
chemicals and pharmaceuticals, electronics and electrical equipment, and

machinery.
Source: LSEG Workspace, Eurostat FIGARO input-output tables, ING
Economic Research
That distinction matters because interruptions in chemicals,
pharmaceuticals, electronics, electrical equipment and critical
materials can quickly aﬀect production further down the supply
chain. These industries are also increasingly exposed to export

### Page 6

controls, subsidies, local-content requirements and other policy
interventions. For ﬁrms operating in this environment, ownership
can provide more control over production, investment and capacity
allocation than an ordinary supply contract.
To understand what is driving this pattern, we next focus on
upstream acquisitions, where companies acquire suppliers or other
ﬁrms at an earlier stage of production. These deals are particularly
relevant to de-risking because they can secure access to critical

inputs.
The sector-level results show that the move towards upstream
ownership is selective. The clearest increase is in critical materials
and metals, where upstream deals rose from 16.5% of acquisitions
in 2015-19 to 20.3% in 2023-25. Energy increased from 4.8% to
8.1%, while chemicals and pharmaceuticals recorded a smaller rise
from 6.1% to 6.7%. By contrast, upstream acquisitions were
broadly stable in electronics and electrical equipment and declined

in machinery.
Upstream ownership is rising mainly in
materials and energy

Economic Research
The two results capture diﬀerent parts of the same development.
Vertical integration has become more common across the

### Page 7

European corporate sector and remains particularly prevalent in
strategic industries. Within those industries, the recent shift
towards acquiring upstream suppliers is concentrated mainly in
resource-related activities.
Raw materials and energy can be diﬃcult to substitute at short
notice, while interruptions can halt production across several
downstream industries. The value of securing access through
ownership is therefore likely to be greatest where the consequences

of losing an input are most severe.
Upstream acquisitions are becoming more
European
Same-country targets remain dominant, accounting for 58.5% of
upstream acquisitions in 2023-2025. That is little changed from
57.8% in 2010-14. There is therefore no clear shift towards purely
domestic ownership.
The more notable development is within the remaining
transactions. The share of targets located in another EU country
increased from 18.1% to 24.8%, while the share outside the EU fell

from 24.1% to 16.7%.
Other EU targets gain share as non-EU
targets decline

### Page 8

Target location in upstream deals by EU non-ﬁnancial ﬁrms, share of deal

Source: LSEG Workspace, Eurostat FIGARO input-output tables, ING
Economic Research
This looks more like Europeanisation of ownership than reshoring.
Domestic targets remain dominant, accounting for 58.5% of
upstream acquisitions in 2023-25, and that share has barely
changed. What has changed is the composition of cross-border
acquisitions: targets elsewhere in the EU have gained share at the

expense of targets outside the bloc. The single market may
therefore oﬀer ﬁrms a way to secure greater control within a
common regulatory framework, without concentrating ownership
in their home country.
What greater corporate control means for
the European economy
The macroeconomic consequences diﬀer from those of reshoring.
Because production is not necessarily being relocated, vertical
integration may leave the geography of employment, output and

trade largely unchanged. Its eﬀects are more likely to arise through
corporate investment, ﬁnancing requirements and the economy’s
exposure to supply shocks.
Acquiring a supplier is considerably more capital-intensive than
adding another supplier or changing a contract. It can give ﬁrms
greater inﬂuence over investment, production and capacity, but
also commits capital that could otherwise ﬁnance innovation or
expansion. The implications for productivity are therefore

### Page 9

ambiguous. Closer coordination may reduce costly interruptions
and encourage supplier-speciﬁc investment, but ownership can
also make it harder to switch suppliers or technologies.
The same trade-oﬀ applies to costs. Greater control can reduce the
risk of acute shortages, but acquisitions, diversiﬁcation, additional
inventories and regional sourcing all carry a price. The emerging
model may therefore produce fewer severe disruptions without
necessarily lowering average production costs.

Ownership is one part of a broader
resilience strategy
Ownership is only one route to greater resilience. Companies can
also add suppliers, deepen contractual relationships, reserve
capacity, or hold more inventories.
Recent evidence from the European Investment Bank suggests
ﬁrms are moving away from some of the emergency responses
used during the pandemic. The share of EU importers adjusting
their supply chains fell from 50% in 2023 to 37% in 2025, while the

use of stockpiling almost halved to 17%. The EIB also ﬁnds that
ﬁrms increasingly rely on supplier and trading-partner
diversiﬁcation.
This helps place the M&A evidence in context. Vertical acquisitions
sit at the most intensive end of a broader adjustment and are likely
to be used where an input is diﬃcult to replace or where greater
inﬂuence over investment and capacity justiﬁes the ﬁnancial
commitment.
Greater resilience will not come cheaply
The emerging supply chain model places greater value on control,

visibility and redundancy. But each response comes with costs.
Acquisitions tie up capital, diversiﬁcation increases procurement
and monitoring expenses, and inventories absorb working capital.
Regional suppliers may be more reliable, but also more expensive.
These costs can be seen as an insurance premium. They may
reduce eﬃciency in normal times, but limit the damage caused by

tariﬀs, export controls, transport disruption or shortages.
For policymakers, the ﬁndings are a reminder that de-risking will

not necessarily appear as reshoring. Companies can change the
ownership and geography of their supply chains without relocating
production. Measures intended to strengthen strategic value chains
may therefore encourage cross-border European investment as well
as domestic production.
European ﬁrms are not retreating from international supply chains
so much as reorganising control within them. The result could be a
more resilient and regionally integrated production model, but not

a costless one: ﬁrms are eﬀectively paying a resilience premium in
exchange for greater control and fewer damaging disruptions.

WEEKLY AGGREGATES
ITEM DEFINITIONS AND METHODOLOGY
3© 2026 LSEG.  All marks herein are used under licence. TRPR_82222_867
General:
All aggregates are bottom-up aggregates calculated from analysts' consensus forecasts unless otherwise noted. Bottom-up
aggregates are weighted calculations using the I/B/E/S data from LSEG share class monitored or the official index weight of
the index-provider. Top-down forecasts are compilations of strategists' estimates. I/B/E/S universe calculations are based on

all companies within a given market monitored by LSEG.
Benchmark index calculations, including those for official local market indices, the S&P 500 Index, and the FTSE World Equity
Indices are calculated using official index constituents and weightings. All index composites are representative of current
index constituents. Index constituents and weightings are updated monthly during options expiration (the Thursday before the
third Friday of every month) for every index except the S&P 500, the constituents of which are current as of the date of

calculation.
PEIGBY refers to the Price Earnings Inverse Government Bond Yield ratio, which measures equity valuation with respect to
the inverse-yield on long-term government bonds (now known as the 'Fed' model). All PEIGBY ratios are I/B/E/S universe-
specific except for the US ratio, where it is based on the S&P 500 Index.
Methodology:
For US and selected Canadian aggregates, calendar-year aggregation is performed using the sum of four quarters' estimates

and a quarterly pro-ration is applied to non-December year end companies' quarterly EPS estimates. Outside of the US and
Canada, all company-level data are calendarized prior to aggregation using the Compustat method of calendarization.
Aggregation is performed using the annual consensus EPS estimate and a May roll-back is applied to December year-end
markets. Aggregates for Japan, where most companies report in March, use an August rollback and a November rollback is

used for Australia and New Zealand, where the fiscal year-end for most companies is in June.
All 12-month-forward data for ex-US markets are calculated using a pro-rata weighting to annual EPS forecasts. The
weightings applied are a daily calibration out of 365 days relative to each constituent's individual fiscal year-end and the
current date.
All non-surprise data, including aggregate forecast earnings, forecast growth, and forward-looking valuation calculations are

weighted according to the I/B/E/S share class monitored for I/B/E/S universes or the weightings assigned to each constituent
in a given index. Aggregated earnings calculations are the equivalent of net profit aggregates, as they are derived from the
EPS forecasts aggregated to the net income level by the appropriate share weighting.
Earnings Aggregation:
For 12-month-forward earnings, the 12-month-forward EPS is used in place of the calendarized EPS in the formulas below.

I/B/E/S universe EPS Aggregate = Index EPS Aggregate =
Growth Calculations:
All growth calculations are the weighted year over year growth in earnings for the calendarized fiscal year period. The
formulas below use index shares for index growth calculations.
Calendarized Earnings Growth = 12-month-forward Growth =
Valuation:
All valuation calculations are the weighted-average price-earnings ratio for the fiscal period in question. The formulas below

use index shares for index P/E calculations.
Calendarized P/E Aggregate = 12-month-forward P/E =
Revisions Momentum:
Unless otherwise indicated, analysts' revisions momentum is a simple aggregation of the total number of estimates raised
divided by the total number of estimates lowered. Thus, any revisions ratio less than 1 indicates negative revisions
momentum while a ratio greater than 1 indicates positive revisions momentum, with 1 marking parity.

The Global Aggregates from LSEG product contains annual aggregates for universes included in this publication, including
supplemental aggregate data of over 100 items as well as history dating back to 1985 for North America and to 1987 for other
markets. To learn more about this product, contact your LSEG sales representative.
FTSE and the FTSE World Equity Indices are owned by FTSE International Limited.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
MIDDLE EAST
BULLETIN
Persistent energy pressures have further to run
19 October 2026
Key points
• Shipping disruptions, depleted
inventories and elevated freight
costs are keeping physical crude
and fuel markets tight despite a

reported recovery in Gulf exports.
• Brent is forecast to average
US$101/bbl in Q4 before easing
to US$77/bbl in Q4 2027.
• Refined product markets are
expected to ease more slowly than
crude, with elevated gasoil cracks,
shipping and insurance costs slowing
declines in petrol and diesel prices.
• Under an escalation scenario,
renewed conflict and deeper supply
disruptions could push Brent towards
US$150/bbl in H1 2027, prolonging
elevated fuel and LNG prices.

• Under an early resolution scenario, a
durable US-Iran agreement accelerates
supply recovery, taking Brent towards
US$65/bbl by Q4 2027 and bringing
fuel and LNG prices down faster.
Q4 2026 forecasts:
Brent crude
US$101/bbl
Japanese LNG
US$24.5/MMBtu

### Page 2

are based are reasonable, the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
2
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
BULLETIN
Low inventories and higher shipping costs will keep oil and
fuel prices elevated, with Brent futures forecast to average
US$101/bbl in Q4 before easing to US$77/bbl by Q4 2027
as supply recovers. Refined fuels and LNG prices should

ease more slowly. Risks are two-sided: escalation could
push Brent towards US$150/bbl, while an early US-Iran
agreement could see a faster normalisation in supply with
prices returning close to pre-conflict levels by end-2027.
This article was first published in the October Market Outlook .
Brent crude is back above US$100/bbl on fading confidence
in a lasting US-Iran agreement and intermittent attacks on
shipping. Buffers remain depleted, with the IEA estimating

global observed inventories were 507mb lower at the end of
August than at the start of the conflict.
This comes despite reported crude exports through the Strait of
Hormuz recovering to around 70% to 90% of pre-conflict levels
in late September. Saudi and UAE exports through the Yanbu and
Fujairah pipelines continue, with the East-West pipeline back to
normal after the early September attacks. Tanker crossings have
risen, although transponders remain switched off, leaving visible

transit at 1% to 4% of pre-conflict levels, while cargo transfers in
the Gulf of Oman have increased as an alternative route.
However, these workarounds are slower and more costly. Ship-
to-ship transfers can take 5 to 10 days, US escorts through
the Gulf via a route close to the Oman shoreline cause delays
while some tankers are taking longer routes. Fewer ships are
also available, with many redeployed to other markets. Higher
insurance premiums are adding to costs, with Gulf-to-China

tanker rates above US$0.9mn a day, around 15x higher than a
year ago. With prompt barrels (available for physical delivery)
scarce, the gap between physical and futures prices widened
again, with Dated Brent around US$136/bbl in early October
against US$101/bbl for front-month futures.
Oil and fuel prices push higher ...
Refined prices have risen more than crude
Refined product markets were already tight going into the
conflict. Reduced Gulf supply, exacerbated by Ukrainian strikes

on Russian refineries, has added to that tightness. Together
with higher shipping costs and rising Dated Brent prices,
Singapore Gasoil 10ppm (diesel) has roughly doubled since the
Strait closed, compared with around 40% for crude futures.
Consequently, Singapore gasoil crack spread widened
to an average of around US$36.1/bbl in early October,
from US$17.5/bbl across 2025. Alongside the Australian
government’s excise tax returning to normal, after it had been

halved, this has lifted national diesel and petrol prices to
around A$2.83/L and A$2.41/L.
Outlook
We do not see a lasting US-Iran agreement until at least
mid-2027. In the meantime, we assume there will not be a
return to full-scale military intervention. However, periodic
Iranian attacks on ships and facilities are expected to continue,
as will the US blockade of Iranian exports, keeping a supply
risk premium in place. We look for Brent futures to average

around US$101/bbl in Q4, then ease gradually as demand
from refiners and governments to rebuild stocks partly offsets
recovering supply. As the premium fades and the market
moves into surplus in late 2027, we forecast Brent at around
US$77/bbl in Q4 2027, easing towards US$70/bbl in 2028 as
Middle East production fully recovers.
Oil balance to gradually improve over 2027
Global oil supply is expected to rise by 4.0mb/d (4.0%) to
103.7mb/d in 2027, following a 6.1% fall in 2026. Most of

this reflects a gradual recovery in Gulf output, with Houthi
Sian Fenner, Head of Business & Industry Economics
Justin Smirk, Senior Economist
Luka Belobajdic, Economist

### Page 3

are based are reasonable, the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
3
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
BULLETIN
attacks on Red Sea shipping keeping Saudi production below
its pre-conflict level until H2 2027. Meanwhile, we look for UAE
production to continue to improve over 2027 and lift above
pre-conflict levels from 2028, as it is no longer constrained by

OPEC quotas. We do not expect a meaningful return of Iranian
oil until an agreement is reached, and damage sustained during
the conflict means production will take time to recover.
We expect global oil demand to be 103.7mb/d in 2026, 0.8mb/d
(0.7%) higher than in 2025, and to rise a further 1.9mb/d (1.8%)
to 105.6mb/d in 2027. Chinese crude imports will continue to
recover over the coming months as refiners restock ahead of
Lunar New Year, but we expect only a gradual return to pre-

conflict levels, as the electrification of transport limits the
recovery in Chinese demand. Globally, demand for diesel and jet
fuel recovers as transport and aviation activity picks up, although
some demand destruction and efficiency gains are expected
to be permanent. The conflict has also highlighted strategic
vulnerabilities, particularly across Asia, so we expect a push to
rebuild reserves adding around 0.5% to demand over 2027.
Fuel prices to ease more slowly than crude

Refined product supply is expected to recover more slowly
than crude, with Gulf refinery runs not returning to pre-war
levels until at least late 2027. Outside the Gulf, there has
been some constructive news. The US has lifted sanctions
on Russian diesel until April 2027, with Russia saying it will
supply more than 4Mt over the coming months, while China
has lifted its ban on refined product exports. However, we
are cautious about how much this will add to the market in

the short term. Much of the Russian supply is headed to the
US and will depend on Russia’s ability to restore refining and
export capacity, while doubts remain over the Russia-Ukraine
energy ceasefire announced by President Trump. China still
manages exports through quotas, which are likely to increase
only gradually as domestic stocks are rebuilt. Refinery runs
should lift ahead of Lunar New Year, but any increase in
exports will take time to reach regional markets.

With product supply recovering slowly and depleted inventories
being rebuilt, Singapore gasoil crack is likely to stay above its
long-run average even as Brent eases, narrowing from US$71/
bbl in Q4 to US$27/bbl by Q4 2027. With Singapore Gas 10ppm
prices, shipping and insurance costs to remain elevated and
easing only gradually in 2027, we forecast Australian diesel
to retail at an average of 274¢/L in Q4, easing to 200¢/L in Q4
2027, and petrol at 230¢/L and 187¢/L respectively.

LNG prices supported through 2027
Japanese LNG prices surged 15.2%mth in September,
averaging US$25.4/MMBtu as supply disruptions in Qatar
and its inability to divert exports around the Strait of Hormuz
tightened markets and intensified competition between
importers. While prices are now more than double pre-conflict
levels, demand destruction across Asia is providing some
offset to tight supply
Our Q4 forecast for Japanese LNG is now US$24.5/mmbtu and

we expect the market to remain structurally tight, with supply
recovery likely to be gradual and prices not returning to pre-
conflict levels until around 2028. Still, Australia’s domestic
gas market should remain insulated, supported by the FY2027
Federal Budget’s domestic gas reservation scheme, which
requires exporters to supply the domestic market with the
equivalent of up to 20% of gas exports. So far this year,
despite the increase in LNG spot prices in Asia, Australian

natural gas prices have remained under the $12 per GJ cap
that has proposed by the Commonwealth government. We are
expecting this to continue well into 2027.
Risks around our oil price outlook are two-sided
Talks are still ongoing in the background, with the US and
Iran exchanging proposals to reopen the Strait, but the gap
between them remains wide. The conflict has also expanded
to the Red Sea and, with inventories sharply drawn down,
there is a smaller buffer to absorb a further shock.

Using the Oxford Economics model, we assess an escalation
scenario, where energy markets face a deeper and more
persistent disruption as the conflict in the Middle East
intensifies. In this case, Brent futures rise to around US$150/
bbl in H1 2027, only dipping to below US$100/bbl in Q2 2028.
We also consider an early resolution scenario, where shipping
and Gulf supply normalise faster, returning Brent to near pre-
conflict levels by end-2027.
Escalation scenario

In the escalation scenario we assume:
Talks stall and the conflict escalates:  the gap between the
US and Iran proves too wide and the US resumes strikes after
the midterms. Iran responds with attacks on shipping and Gulf
energy infrastructure, damaging export, storage and refining
facilities. A lasting agreement is not reached until 2028.
Export routes are disrupted: more frequent attacks curtail
dark crossings of the Strait and ship-to-ship transfers in the

Gulf of Oman. Houthi attacks also cut off Saudi alternative
routes, forcing Gulf producers to cut output and Middle
East crude exports sharply lower. New routes and shipping
arrangements eventually support a recovery in Gulf flows, but
trade patterns take time to adjust.
... and risks remain and while they are two sided ...

are based are reasonable, the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
4
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
Product supply fails to recover:  The Russia–Ukraine energy
ceasefire does not hold and strikes on Russian refineries
continue, limiting how much diesel Russia can export,
even with the US sanctions waiver extended. China also

reintroduces its export ban to protect domestic supply.
Oil prices lift to around US$150/bbl in H1 2027: With little
buffer to absorb the supply loss, competition for physical
delivery intensifies, with physical prices well above futures
as prompt barrels remain scarce. Higher prices support
supply growth outside OPEC and drive demand destruction,
alongside further emergency stock releases, but a deeper
and more prolonged global deficit remains. Brent stays above

US$100/bbl until mid-2028 as governments rebuild reserves.
The Singapore gasoil crack widens and, with freight and
insurance costs also higher, Australian diesel and petrol prices
average around 247 cpl and 231 cpl at end 2027. Japanese
LNG also remains elevated around US$23.2/MMBtu in Q4
2027, $9.1 above our baseline.
Early resolution scenario
In the early resolution scenario we assume:
A durable agreement: The US and Iran agree terms to reopen
the Strait by the end of year, and, unlike the June interim deal,

the agreement holds, with progress towards a permanent
settlement in early 2027. Attacks on shipping cease and the
US blockade is lifted, allowing Iranian oil back into the market,
initially from storage. Vessels are quickly repositioned to the
Gulf, with freight and war-risk insurance easing from early
2027 rather than mid-year.
A faster recovery in supply:  With export routes secure, Gulf
production and damaged refinery and export capacity recover

sooner than in the baseline. Outside the Gulf, the US extends
its waiver on Russian diesel beyond April 2027 and China
raises export quotas as domestic stocks are rebuilt.
Demand settles below pre-conflict trend: Some demand
destruction and efficiency gains persist, particularly in China.
With inventories rebuilding faster, government restocking is
smaller and more gradual than in the baseline.
Prices fall quickly, but restocking provides a floor:  Brent

Brent futures ease to around US$88/bbl in Q1 2027 as the
premium for prompt physical barrels unwinds. As the market
moves into balance, Brent eases to around US$65/bbl by Q4
2027, close to pre-conflict levels.
Refined product prices ease faster on lower freight costs with
the Singapore gasoil crack narrowing. Australian diesel and
petrol prices fall to around 182 cpl and 170 cpl by Q4 2027. Gas
markets also ease, although damage to Qatar’s gas facility means

export capacity recovers gradually. Japanese LNG falls to around
US$11.9/MMBtu by end-2027, $2.1 below our baseline.
Economic impact
In the escalation scenario, higher fuel costs flow through to
transport, food and other energy-intensive goods such as
plastics and fertilisers. Australian headline CPI remains above
4.5% for much of 2027, with underlying inflation remaining
sticky at above 3% until late 2028. The RBA faces a difficult
trade off between much higher inflation and a significant

hit to activity, as households cut discretionary spending and
businesses defer investment. Higher LNG export earnings
provide only a moderate offset. In the early resolution scenario,
headline inflation is around 0.1ppt below the baseline in 2027.
New Zealand is more exposed in both scenarios, with food
and energy a larger share of its CPI and no offsetting energy
exports. Headline inflation is around 1ppt higher in the
escalation scenario in Q1 2027.

... the upside presents a more dramatic shift in prices

Document 372: Postcard from Bangkok IMF meetings_ don’t take resilience for granted (2026-10-19)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Postcard from Bangkok IMF meetings_ don’t take resilience for granted (2026-10-19).pdf
Report source: BNP Paribas
Date: 2026-10-19
Pages: 2
Status: ok

### Page 1

The bank
for a changing
world
3
economic-research.bnpparibas.comEcoWeek 26.38 / 19 October 2026EDITORIAL
POSTCARD FROM BANGKOK IMF MEETINGS: DON’T TAKE RESILIENCE FOR GRANTED
1 See EcoNews for more details.
The global economy’s unexpected resilience might have invited the self-congratulation that is a trademark of internatio-
nal official meetings. But policymakers gathered in Bangkok for the 2026 IMF Annual Meetings were wearily aware that
“continued resilience cannot be taken for granted”, as noted in the prologue to the closing Chair’s Statement to the Inter-

national Monetary and Financial Committee meeting. Growth forecasts remain in line with those made a year ago, when
interest rates were much lower and no energy shock was in the cards, and even with those made in April, when a short
shock was the base case. But what has sustained global growth against the odds so far could falter before the headwinds
ease: oil buffers are being depleted; fiscal policy urgently needs to shift from support to deficit reduction as interest rates

remain persistently higher; and the AI boom that has been a salvatory growth engine comes with serious questions about
its sustainability and long-term economic effects. In addition to these global issues, the combination of fiscal challenges
and elections in France and the US were seen as key risks, while some topics that had until recently been in the spotlight
found themselves overshadowed.
BETTER GROWTH THAN EXPECTED FOR MOST , WORSE INFLATION FOR ALL

Despite the energy shock and surge in interest rates, the IMF's
latest global growth forecast is unchanged from a year ago and April
— at 3.1% in 2026 and 3.4% in 2027 — although the geographical
breakdown has shifted: war-exposed countries and many energy im -
porters have been downgraded, while AI-exposed countries have been
upgraded. Asia is the standout winner, expected to account for 68% of
global growth in 2026 — a feat for a region highly dependent on Gulf

energy. Compared to July, the growth forecast for advanced economies
is revised up to 2.1% in 2026 (+0.4pp, driven by Germany) and remains
at 1.8% in 2027. Among emerging markets, the IMF upgraded Asia and
downgraded other regions. Meanwhile, global headline inflation is ex-
pected to rise to 4.7% in 2026, mainly due to energy and food, before
easing to 4.1% in 2027 (revised +0.2pp)1.
AI OPTIMISM DOMINATES, BUT WITH SOME HEAVY UNANSWERED
QUESTIONS

The topic of AI dominated the Meetings. The broad consensus was that
it is transformational and offers great opportunities to all countries.
But, for now, it is producing a mixed bag of economic effects: a power-
ful boost to global demand, directly through an investment and trade
boom and indirectly through wealth effects from stock-market gains;
but also much higher interest rates (fuelled by the borrowing needed
to fund the AI build-out, and higher prices for chips, all the products

they go into, and the electricity and water data centres need).
Beyond this shared diagnosis, strong arguments could be heard on both
sides of several critical questions. The most pressing being: will AI ge-
nerate earnings commensurate with current expectations? If not — or
even if expectations are merely downgraded — expect large declines in
AI-related asset prices, a sudden stop in funding and a significant blow
to growth wherever AI has been a key engine. This is not in anyone’s

baseline for next year, but it is clearly a top risk. To what extent will AI
boost productivity over five or ten years? The IMF incorporates almost
no boost in its five-year baseline, but presents an upside scenario in
which it does. Will AI create more jobs than it destroys, raise or reduce
labour’s share of income, prove inflationary or deflationary beyond the
near term, and strengthen cyber resilience or turbocharge attacks?
And how should it be taxed to preserve the fiscal base and offset unde-

sirable labour-market effects?
A MIXED POLICY SCORECARD
Monetary policy has appropriately taken a hawkish turn nearly eve -
rywhere, and an increase in rate rises is likely in key economies, though
not enough to threaten growth. Eurozone central bankers were parti -
cularly clear in sticking to their “moderate response” playbook.
Fiscal policy, however, has yet to adjust to the end of cheap debt. With
public debt high in most countries, a growing share of national bud -

gets will be absorbed by interest payments. Yet very few countries are
consolidating. The IMF’s top call to action was to address this urgent-
ly, especially among the worst offenders — the US and France. Fiscal
restraint would also help central banks curb inflation, reducing the
need for higher interest rates.
Policies to redress global imbalances are not closer to being adop -
ted than at previous IMF Meetings, but they were not particular -
ly highlighted by the IMF this time, beyond the mantra that “the US

needs to save more, China to consume more, and Europe to invest
more”. There were reportedly good discussions behind closed doors
— at the G7 on “non-market policies and practices (NMPPs)” — and
encouraging early steps in a long journey in Europe-China talks. That
is better than nothing, but hardly enough to expect progress within a
reasonable timeframe. Meanwhile, the risks of a disorderly unwind will
keep rising.
TWO ZONES OF CONCERN IN FOCUS: FRANCE AND THE US

France was a top concern for many participants. They feared a repeat
of the 2010–2012 Eurozone debt crisis, seeing a country with public
debt approaching 120% of GDP , sub-par growth, a poor record of fiscal
consolidation, riots in the streets and presidential elections six mon -
ths away, with populist candidates leading the polls. French and Eu -
ropean policymakers, alongside private-sector participants, presented
a different picture, namely: a fiscal situation that is serious, but is

being managed (the draft budget provides for meaningful adjustment
and is very likely to be adopted without being weakened); a widening
consensus from centre-left to far-right which favours a return to fiscal
responsibility, in line with a similar shift in public opinion; and the
electoral process is only beginning, with the outcome still wide open —
not least because the candidates’ names and numbers are not known.

### Page 2

4
economic-research.bnpparibas.comEcoWeek 26.38 / 19 October 2026
The bank
for a changing
world
It was also pointed out that the quantum of fiscal adjustment required
is not out of line with previous consolidation episodes in France in the
2010s and the 1990s. Still, most participants left not much reassured
and expecting a bumpy ride ahead.
US participants outside government saw a growing likelihood of a De -
mocratic sweep in the midterm elections, Either way, they expected

little fiscal consolidation, despite a manifestly challenging fiscal path
that may end up causing volatility in financial markets. Non-US parti -
cipants also feared growing policy unpredictability.
OVERSHADOWED TOPICS: THE STARS OF RECENT IMF MEETINGS
Tariffs were entirely absent from the agenda and conversations. Toke-
nised finance (rather than the narrower topic of stablecoins) still fea -
tured, but far less prominently: as the field takes shape, discussion

has shifted from the conceptual what and why to the technical how.
Climate change and low-income countries remained on the agenda,
but far less in focus than at previous Meetings. The sheer number of
issues demanding attention may be overwhelming financial policyma-
kers’ bandwidth.
*****
All in all, while the global context remains extraordinarily challenging,
it’s comforting to see that global financial policy makers are broadly
alert to both risks and opportunities, and to what needs to be done for

the latter to prevail. Now they need to deliver.
Isabelle Mateos y Lago
isabelle.mateosylago@bnpparibas.com

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
BULLETIN
August inflation came in soft...
Inflation moderated in August following July’s strong 
outcome. The CPI rose 0.4% in the month, in line with our 
expectation but slightly below market consensus of 0.5%, 
taking the annual rate to 4.0%. On a seasonally adjusted 
basis, the CPI increased 0.7%mth, underscoring the impact of 
seasonal discounting in August and the start of the financial 
year repricing in July.
More closely watched are the measures of underlying inflation 
including the Trimmed Mean (TM) and market services. 
The TM rose 0.2% in August, in line with our expectations 
but again softer than the market, leaving the annual pace 
unchanged at 3.6% for a third consecutive month. Meanwhile, 
market services excluding volatiles & holiday travel increased 
0.2%mth/4.1%yr. Notable strength (three-month annualised, 
seasonally adjusted) was seen in sports participation (10.9%), 
clothing repair (7.0%), restaurant meals (6.8%) and other 
household services (5.2%). For full analysis of the August CPI, 
see “August CPI – Came in on our expectations ”.
The expenditure-weighted share of the CPI basket 
experiencing positive price shocks eased to 11% in August 
from 15% in July, while the share experiencing negative price 
shocks declined from 17% to just below 13%. The majority of 
the positive shocks were concentrated in consumer durables 
On a three-month moving average basis, the net momentum 
share (positive minus negative) rose to –4.5% from –7.7% 
in July and a trough of –15.8% in May, suggesting the 
disinflationary impulse may be nearing exhaustion. 
... with September due for a moderate pick-up
Looking ahead to September, early indicators painted a 
mixed picture. The NAB business survey showed retail price 
growth slowing to 0.9%qtr from 1.1%qtr in August, with 
labour cost growth easing to 1.7%qtr from 1.8%qtr, while 
purchase cost growth edged up to 2.4%qtr from 2.3%qtr. 
Nevertheless, all measures remain well above their long-run 
averages while the gap between purchase costs and final 
prices continues to widen, indicating businesses are facing 
margin compression (see here for further detail). Meanwhile, 
consumers’ year-ahead inflation expectations held steady 
at 4.9% in September (easing slightly at 2dp) but surged to 
5.3% in October likely reflecting higher fuel prices, with retail 
petrol and diesel averaging $2.20/l (+8.2%mth) and $2.68/l 
(+7.7%mth). We do believe the auto fuel prices are likely to see 
a near term peak in October.  Meanwhile, global shipping costs 
have also surged recently amid renewed Middle East tensions.
Sep quarter unrevised but Dec upgraded significantly
Justin Smirk, Senior Economist
Neha Sharma, Economist

The September CPI is expected to rise 0.6% (0.62% to 2dp)
with the annual pace lifting to 4.1%yr. Our seasonally adjusted
estimate increases 0.5% (0.48% to 2dp). For the monthly
TM, we expect a 0.3% (0.26% to 2dp) lift, holding the annual

pace steady at 3.6%. On a 3m/3m basis, the TM is tracking
a 1.0% gain in September (noting that while this measure is
conceptually different to the quarterly TM it still serves as a
useful guide). For market services excluding volatiles & holiday
travel we have pencilled in another 0.2% rise, slowing the
annual pace slightly to 4.0% (a nine-month low).
The largest contributors are recreation & culture (+0.27ppt),
driven wholly by holiday travel & accommodation. Transport

(+0.23ppt) makes a solid contribution, with gains in fuel
(+0.26ppt) partly offset by motor vehicles (–0.02ppt). Housing
also contributes (+0.13ppt), with rents and new dwellings
each adding 0.02ppt while property rates add 0.08ppt.
The key detractors in the month are food & non-alcoholic
beverages (–0.08ppt) and garments (–0.04ppt).
Key details for the month of September
Food & non-alcoholic beverages are expected to fall
0.5%mth, easing the annual pace to 2.8%yr. The weakness is

concentrated in a 3.7%mth decline in fruit & vegetables, while
non-alcoholic beverages are also expected to fall 1.5%mth.
Meals out & takeaway is forecast to rise 0.1%mth, easing the
annual rate to 3.9%.
Housing is expected to rise 0.6% in September, slowing the
annual pace to 5.5%yr. Component-wise:
• Rents are expected to rise 0.3%mth and 3.7%yr. CPI rent
inflation is nearing its peak, consistent with the moderation
in asking rent growth. We expect rent inflation to ease to

3.6%yr by end-2026 and 2.5%yr by end-2027.
• New dwelling prices, which capture only the building
component of construction costs, are expected to rise
0.2%mth and 4.9%yr. Ongoing weakness in established
housing markets would be increasing builders’ difficulty in
passing through higher costs to new builds. As a result, we
expect new dwelling inflation to moderate to 4.5%yr by
end-2026 and 2.1%yr by end-2027.
Transport inflation is expected to rise 2.0%mth in September,

lifting the annual pace to 7.7%yr reflecting an 8.3%mth
increase in fuel prices following higher oil prices through the
month. Motor vehicle prices and spare parts are expected to
fall 0.7%mth and 1.3%mth, respectively. In NSW, a one-year
vehicle registration discount between $80-100 came into
effect from 1 September, which is likely to weigh on the other
services in respect of motor vehicles component.
Recreation & culture is expected to rise 2.1%mth driven by a

4.4%mth increase in holiday travel, partly offset by a 0.4%
decline in audio, visual & computing equipment & services and
a 1.3% fall in sporting services.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
Risks to September baseline
We view the risks around the September CPI outcome as broadly 
balanced. Waning spending momentum outside of fuel may make 
it difficult for businesses to pass through higher costs in the near 
term. However, some of these pricing pressures may be deferred 
rather than forgone. The October ban on card surcharging may 
prompt a greater-than-usual degree of price resetting, allowing 
businesses to recover some of the margin pressures that have built 
up in recent months.
Risks to housing are mixed. Trade materials suppliers continued 
to announce above-average price increases through September 
(around 8%). While soft conditions in the established housing 
market may limit the extent to which these costs are passed 
through to new dwelling prices, they nevertheless present an 
upside risk to housing-related inflation, particularly maintenance 
& repair and insurance costs. Growth in advertised rents also 
continues to ease but these new leases take some time to flow 
through to average rents paid measured in the CPI. 
A kick up in October ...
We have a solid preliminary estimate for the October CPI of 
0.6%mth lifting the annual pace to 4.8%yr. It is true that the key 
driver is an 8.9% increase in auto fuel but we estimate the TM to 
lift 0.4%mth/3.7%yr highlighting broader inflationary pressures. 
We expect that the removal of credit charge surcharges and 
resulting increase in retail prices, at most, will contribute 0.1ppt 
but only just so see it as more of a rounding error. Nevertheless, 
these changes come at a time of a significant upstream price 
pressure, particularly for SMEs, so there is some risk of broader 
price adjustments. As such, we expect marginally larger than 
usual price adjustments in October particularly in hospitality 
and household services. Being the first month of the quarter the 
October lift sets a higher base for the quarterly average estimates 
resulting in an upgrade to our December quarter estimate.
We have included a table with a monthly profile for the CPI, 
seasonally adjusted CPI and the TM out to June 2027. At 
June 2027 we have an annual pace of 3.7%yr for the CPI and 
seasonally adjusted CPI and 3.3%yr for the TM. There is also a 
table breaking down the monthly components of the CPI through 
the December quarter. 
... highlights stronger inflationary pressure ...

was 1.9%, contributing 0.55ppt to the 0.8ppt increase in the 
quarterly estimate. Outside of a 0.09ppt contribution to the 
revision from holiday travel & accommodation (revised up from 
4.2% to 5.6%) all else was via small contributions for a large 
number of expenditure categories. This is why there is a 0.2ppt 
increase in our September quarter TM estimate from 0.7%qtr 
to 0.9%qtr.
A disinflationary impulse kicks in for 2027
We now expect auto fuel prices to peak in October (240 cpl 
for petrol, 284 cpl for diesel) and then to trend lower through 
2027 (184 cpl for petrol and 194 cpl for diesel by December 
2027) leading to a 13.6% falling in auto fuel in the first half 
of 2027 subtracting 0.46ppt off the CPI over the same period 
of time. For a detailed description on how we constructed the 
auto fuels profile please see: “ Persistent energy pressures 
have further to run ” 
The first half of 2027 will also see an almost 5% decline in 
holiday travel & accommodation which will subtract a further 
0.29ppt off the CPI. However, outside of that any broader 
moderation in inflation is expected to be more muted hence 
we have the TM printing 0.7%qtr for each quarter in 2027.  
Critical to this moderation in core inflation is our expect step 
down in dwelling price inflation on the back of falling estab -
lished home prices. From a 1.1%qtr increase in Q3 2026 we 
see dwelling price increasing by 0.7% in the Q4 2026, and 
then 0.6% in both Q1 and Q2 2027 before dropping to 0.5%qtr 
in Q3 and Q4 2027. This will see dwelling price inflation in the 
CPI drop from 5.4%yr at June 2026 to 3.0%yr by June 2027 
then down to 2.2%yr by December 2027. 
At least for the near term a key uncertainty remains the extent 
to which businesses are able to pass through ongoing labour 
and non-labour cost pressures and how this unwinds through 
2027 as energy prices ease and the domestic economy slows 
under the weight of higher interest rates and falling estab -
lished home prices. There is a risk that firms face greater 
resistance to price increases if discretionary household spend -
ing softens by more than expected. That said, these headwinds 
may be at least partially offset by the stronger starting point 
for household incomes, which has proven more resilient than 
previously anticipated, and by higher energy prices due to the 
ongoing conflict in the Middle East.
That said, there is also the risk that the disinflationary pulse by 
the second half of 2027 could be greater than what we antici-

above their pre-conflict level. Should all these factors improve

level if not lower, then headline inflation could dip below 2%yr 
and the TM would be closer to 2.5%yr (the mid point if the 
RBA’s inflation target), by end 2027.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
EXECUTIVE SUMMARY
Markets have had little respite over the past month. Hopes 
of a lasting US–Iran agreement have faded and intermittent 
attacks on shipping have kept Brent oil prices in triple digits. 
Workarounds have allowed a meaningful share of crude to 
continue flowing but these arrangements are slower and 
costlier, while depleted inventories leave little room to 
absorb another shock and a range of disruptions to refining 
and shipping are also impacting downstream energy costs. 
Bond markets have been just as restless, with continued lifts 
in government bond yields across much of the globe and 
more sharply in the US. 
Notably, oil is no longer doing the heavy lifting for yields. 
The rise has been concentrated in real yields, with the Fed’s 
September hike – and its signal of more to come – acting as 
something of a trigger. In the US, fiscal concerns have been 
more of a sub-plot, though the Treasury’s efforts to hold 
down long yields have arguably only drawn more attention 
to them. 
In our view, markets are getting ahead of themselves on 
monetary policy offshore. We expect just one more hike 
from the Fed rather than the three the market currently has 
priced, with stretched housing affordability posing risks to 
both consumption and inflation. Central banks in Europe, the 
UK and Canada are also likely to bide their time after a final 
move. Europe is weathering the energy shock better than 
feared and has far more fiscal room to respond than the US – 
one reason we still see the US dollar trending lower over the 
next two years.
The RBA hiked again in September and looks set to follow 
up in November, taking the cash rate to a new cycle high. 
Some of the risks the Board flagged have crystallised, 
but the minutes also reveal a tendency to reach for the 
more hawkish read of the data, in addition to the familiar 
downbeat take on supply capacity. Our base case assumes 
no return to full-scale conflict, but with energy buffers 
depleted, bond markets less forgiving and the RBA already 
leaning hawkish, the margin for error – for markets and 
policymakers alike – is starting to ‘wear thin’.
Australia: After a soft but better than expected first half, the 
Australian economy is facing into more challenges as another 
round of disruptions from global energy markets combine with 
further interest rate rises and a deepening housing downturn. 
The demand mix should see capacity pressures continue to 
ease but with significant risks on either side.
Middle East: Low inventories and higher shipping costs will 
keep oil and fuel prices elevated, with Brent futures forecast 
to average US$101/bbl in Q4 before easing to US$77/bbl by 
Q4 2027 as supply recovers. Risks are two-sided: escalation 
could push Brent towards US$150/bbl, while an early US-Iran 
agreement could see a faster normalisation in supply.
Commodities: Most commodities rose in September, with 
many of the themes that drove moves across August remaining 
in place. Japanese LNG and Brent oil recorded strong gains 
amid Middle East tensions. Copper and aluminium extended 
recent gains while iron ore was supported by higher freight 
and production costs. Gold fell after the Fed’s September rate 
hike. 
Global FX Markets: In late September and early-October 
the DXY index popped higher to near 103, a high back to 
early-2025. Underlying the move are expectations that 
inflation will prove persistent globally as well as a belief that 
the US’ tech sector leaves it best positioned to weather the 
storm.
New Zealand: Higher fuel costs and a weaker exchange rate 
have lifted the short-term inflation outlook while the economy 
has continued to grow through the Iran war. Growth looks set 
to continue to be buoyed by strong commodity prices and a 
supportive exchange rate. However, downside risks are growing 
from higher energy costs and long-term interest rates. 
United States: The US housing market remains under 
considerable pressure. Prices continue to edge up, supporting 
wealth. But up-and-coming main street consumers are 
struggling with affordability and availability, posing enduring 
risks for both consumption growth and inflation.   
China: With each month that passes, the risk of disappointing 
authorities’ 4.5-5.0% ambition grows. While the Government 
is right to take a targeted approach to support, it must quickly 
multiply its scale if downside risks are to be kept at bay. 
Asia: For much of the past twenty years, Asia has been a 
source of disinflation for the world. But a combination of ageing 
populations, rising labour costs, increasing electricity demand 
and technology sector developments imply Asia may provide 
less support to global disinflation over the decade ahead.
Europe & UK: The euro has come under considerable pressure 
during the past month. That said, the European economy 
continues to tick along nicely while inflation risks remain 
manageable, allowing the ECB to be measured in their response.

Wearing thin

### Page 4

the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
FINANCIAL MARKETS
The main development affecting global markets this month
has been the sharp lift in selected bond yields, especially in
the US and France. Since the previous Market Outlook was
published, 10-year benchmarks have risen 27bps for US

Treasuries, to be well above the 5% threshold, and 40bps for
French bonds. The latter move contrasts with German bunds,
which have seen little change in yields at that tenor over the
month, though they are up more than 60bps year to date.
Several factors are behind the latest rise, with no single
cause dominating. For some of these factors, the underlying
conditions had been in evidence for some time, but recent
events focused attention on them. The rise has been primarily

a story of real yields: longer-run inflation expectations implied
by both breakevens and swaps have not increased that much.
For much of the year, fluctuations in oil prices – and thus the
near-term inflation outlook – fed into market views of central
bank policy, and longer yields as well. More recently, though,
this correlation has broken down. Shifts in expectations about
central bank policy have remained important for bond pricing,
as evident in the concentration of the increase in real yields.

The FOMC’s decision to increase the Fed funds rate, and flag in
the ‘dot plots’ that at least one more hike was likely, seems to
have been something of a trigger event for repricing, not only
for US yields. Also salient were the various actions taken by
the US Treasury to suppress long yields and improve the US
government’s debt-servicing position. These efforts had little
lasting effect and yields are now higher, and higher relative to
peers, than before. But they may have served to draw attention

to the growing issues around US fiscal sustainability. High
supply of debt from hyperscaler firms to fund the AI / data
centre boom has also likely pressured yields at the margin.
That said, fiscal concerns are unlikely to have been the main
issue driving the lift in US yields. The slope of the US yield
curve is noticeably flatter than at the beginning of the year;
in other words, short yields have risen even more than the
10-year yield, signifying tighter expected monetary policy

lifting yields across the curve. Another lens on this shift is that
2-year and other short-dated yields have increased more than
estimates of risk-neutral rate, such as that from the NY Fed.
Fiscal concerns have been more salient in the case of France,
where spreads to bunds have widened from around 70bps at
the beginning of the year to nearly 140bps currently, wider than
Greece or Italy, with about 40bps of the widening occurring in
the past month. Contagion to other euro area sovereigns has

been limited, with spreads widening around 22bps over the
month for Greece, Italy and Belgium, and 13bps for Spain.
Many factors lifting global bond yields ...
Luci Ellis
Chief Economist, Westpac Group
